Optimization of sustainable supply chains under uncertainty: integrating Internet of Things and blockchain
Authors: Sepideh Chehreh, Mahdi Rezaei, Ali Salmasnia
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
In today’s competitive environment, emerging technologies such as the Internet of Things and blockchain can enhance transparency, traceability, and information management in supply chains. Based on this perspective, a multi-period and multi-product virtual closed-loop supply chain optimization model is developed. In this model, IoT and blockchain are incorporated as traceability and information-management infrastructures that facilitate product tracking and information transparency across the supply chain. The model is formulated as a mixed-integer linear programming model with a four-echelon forward structure and three-echelon reverse structure. Demand uncertainty is managed using a fuzzy approach. The problem is solved for small- and medium-scale instances using GAMS. For large-scale instances, due to NP-hard nature of the problem, the Grey Wolf Optimizer algorithm is used. To examine the role of Internet of Things and blockchain technologies in the supply chain, two comparative studies are conducted: the first study analyzes the simultaneous implementation of these two technologies. The second study focuses on the role of blockchain. The results of the comparative studies indicate that integrating the Internet of Things and blockchain enhances supply chain sustainability, transparency, and profitability.

Introduction

This section first presents the background and motivation of the study, followed by the identification of the main research gaps and motivation in the existing literature. Then, the key contributions of the study are presented, and finally, the structure of the paper is described.

Background and motivation

Digital transformation and environmental pressures, expanding global markets and intensified competition have significantly increased the complexity of supply chains (SCs) design and management. This complexity is particularly evident in closed-loop supply chains (CLSCs)1,2. CLSCs play a critical role in implementing circular economy principles by enabling product recovery, reuse, and recycling3. These SCs face numerous challenges such as demand uncertainty, coordination complexity, sustainability considerations, and the need for efficient recycling. Therefore, given their inherent complexity and uncertainty, CLSCs require advanced modeling and intelligent management4.

To address these challenges, advances in information technology have transformed the structure and performance of SCs5. Emerging technologies such as blockchain and the Internet of Things (IoT) have significant transformative potential. In particular, the IoT provides real-time data acquisition and traceability capabilities that support product monitoring and information visibility across the SC6–8. Meanwhile, blockchain establishes transparency, trust, and decentralized data integrity, thereby supporting secure information sharing and traceability across SC stakeholders9,10. Together, these capabilities, particularly within the context of the fourth industrial revolution and digital transformation play a vital role in rapid adaptation and competitive advantage11–13.

Despite these technological advancements, integrating them into a comprehensive and practical mathematical model remains a fundamental challenge. This model must address real-world business characteristics simultaneously. These characteristics include multi-product structures, multi-period planning, demand uncertainty, and conflicting economic and sustainability objectives. This gap remains a fundamental challenge and a significant gap in the existing literature14,15.

In other words, although many technological tools are available, their integration into a unified decision-making framework remains limited. As a result, in practice, decision-makers lack integrated models that combine multi-product and multi-period structures with IoT- and blockchain-enabled traceability and information-management infrastructures under uncertain environments. This limitation creates a gap between theoretical developments and practical implementation in CLSC design. Building on the above discussion, a more detailed analysis of the literature is required to clearly identify the existing research gaps.

Research gap and motivation

Building on the above discussion, recent advancements in CLSC design have significantly focused on sustainability integration and the adoption of emerging digital technologies such as the IoT and blockchain16–18. Despite these developments, several important gaps remain in the literature, limiting both the theoretical advancement and practical applicability of existing CLSC models19. Identifying and addressing these gaps is essential because modern SCs operate in highly dynamic, uncertain, and technology-intensive environments that require integrated decision-support frameworks capable of balancing operational, technological, and strategic considerations20.

First, a substantial body of CLSC research is based on restrictive assumptions. These include single-product configurations, single-period planning horizons, and deterministic environments21,22. While these assumptions simplify model formulation, they fail to fully capture the inherent complexity of real-world SCs23. Real systems are typically multi-product, multi-period, and subject to demand uncertainty24. Consequently, the practical applicability of such models remains limited25. From a theoretical perspective, oversimplified assumptions may lead to conclusions that do not adequately reflect the interactions among products, facilities, and decision variables across multiple planning periods26. From a managerial perspective, decision-makers require models that can effectively respond to fluctuating demand, changing market conditions, and operational disruptions27. Therefore, developing more realistic CLSC models that incorporate these characteristics remains an important research priority.

Second, although IoT and blockchain technologies have been widely studied in SC management, most studies consider them separately rather than within an integrated framework28,29. IoT is primarily used for real-time data acquisition and traceability, whereas blockchain ensures transparency and data integrity30. These technologies possess complementary capabilities because IoT continuously generates operational data from products, assets, and logistics activities, while blockchain provides a secure and decentralized platform for storing, validating, and sharing this information among supply chain stakeholders31. The integration of these technologies can support end-to-end visibility, product and material traceability, improved information sharing, and greater trust among participants32. Such capabilities provide valuable informational support for traceability, transparency, and information management across the SC and contribute to greater visibility and coordination across SC stakeholders33. However, the absence of an integrated approach limits the full exploitation of their complementary capabilities in enhancing SC visibility and information transparency34. When IoT and blockchain are implemented independently, organizations may face fragmented information flows, reduced data consistency, limited transparency, and lower confidence in information records and traceability outcomes35. As a result, the potential benefits of digital transformation cannot be fully realized, highlighting the need for integrated CLSC frameworks that incorporate the complementary functionalities of both technologies in supporting traceability, transparency, and information management within CLSC.

Third, only a limited number of studies simultaneously address multi-echelon network design, stochastic uncertainty, and investment decisions related to digital infrastructure within a unified CLSC framework16,17,36. In practice, digital transformation requires more than operational optimization37. It also requires the explicit consideration of investment and security aspects, which are often overlooked in existing models, this limitation is particularly important because the implementation of IoT devices, blockchain platforms, communication infrastructure, and cybersecurity mechanisms involves substantial financial commitments and long-term strategic planning38. Furthermore, concerns regarding data security, privacy protection, cyber risks, and system reliability have become increasingly critical as SCs become more digitally connected39. Neglecting these factors may result in solutions that are operationally efficient but economically infeasible or insufficiently secure for real-world implementation40. Therefore, integrating investment planning and security considerations into CLSC design represents an important theoretical and practical research challenge that remains insufficiently addressed in the existing literature41.

Overall, these limitations collectively indicate that current CLSC models remain fragmented and insufficiently aligned with real-world requirements. More specifically, existing studies often focus on isolated dimensions of SC design while overlooking the interdependencies among operational complexity, technological integration, uncertainty management, investment planning, and security considerations14. This fragmentation reduces the ability of current models to support comprehensive decision-making in modern digital SCs42. Therefore, there is a clear need for an integrated and realistic modeling framework. This framework should incorporate multi-product, multi-period, stochastic conditions, and IoT–blockchain environments. By addressing these interconnected dimensions simultaneously, researchers can develop more robust decision-support tools that better reflect the realities of contemporary SCs and provide stronger managerial insights. This study addresses these gaps by developing a comprehensive CLSC model that bridges the gap between theoretical modeling and practical implementation. Specifically, the proposed framework incorporates IoT- and blockchain-enabled traceability and information-management infrastructures alongside operational decisions, investment planning, and threat-risk management under uncertainty, thereby contributing to both the theoretical development of CLSC modeling and the practical implementation of digital SC transformation. To address these identified gaps, the contributions of this study are presented in the following section.

Contributions of the study

This study proposes a novel modeling framework to overcome the aforementioned gaps, with the following contributions:Development of a comprehensive CLSC model: In this study, a multi-product, multi-period, and multi-echelon model is presented for the design of a virtual closed-loop supply chain network (VCLSCN) under demand uncertainty.Integration of IoT technology into the SC structure: IoT technology is incorporated into the model to enable real-time data collection ans support the tracking of product flows throughout the SC. In addition, it serves as a traceability and information-acquisition infrastructure that facilitates the monitoring of returned products and enhances visibility across the SC.Integration of blockchain technology into the SC structure: Blockchain is considered in the model as a decentralized and secure infrastructure to ensure transparency, immutability, and data reliability. It also enhances the credibility of the data collected through the IoT. This integration enhances transparency, data integrity, and the reliability of information records across SC stakeholders.Consideration of investment in digital infrastructure security: Investments aimed at countering potential threats are incorporated into the model to ensure the security of the SC’s digital infrastructures.Capability to solve large-scale problems using the Grey Wolf Optimizer (GWO) algorithm: Given the NP-hard nature of the model, a GWO algorithm is developed to solve large-scale problems. In this regard, to validate the developed algorithm, its results are compared with those obtained from the CPLEX solver in GAMS for small- and medium-sized instances.

Structure of the paper

The remainder of this paper is organized as follows: “Background and literature review” section presents the background and literature review, including emerging technologies in SCs, sustainability aspects, information security considerations, and deterministic and stochastic SC models. “Problem definition” section describes the problem definition and model assumptions. “Model description” section introduces the proposed mathematical model, including the objective function, cost functions, constraints, and the uncertainty management approach. “Numerical example” section validates the significant impact of employing the IoT and blockchain on profitability and the demand response rate in SC. “Discussion” section provides a structured interpretation of the results from theoretical, practical, managerial, and decision-making perspectives. Finally, “Conclusion” section concludes the paper by summarizing the main findings and outlining directions for future research.

Background and literature review

In this section, a systematic literature review is conducted in accordance with the framework proposed in “Systematic scientific research management”43. To better understand the research context, it is necessary to examine the structural configurations of SCs and the role of emerging digital technologies in transforming these systems.

In the literature, SCs are commonly classified based on their structure into forward SCs and CLSCs. In addition, from a technology perspective, SCs can be categorized into traditional (non-digital) systems and virtual supply chains (VSCs) enabled by intelligent technologies. This dual classification provides a useful framework for analyzing recent developments in SC design and management.

In relation to the first category of studies, Wang et al.29 examined a forward SC that coordinates customers, robots, and cloud computing. They employed advanced algorithms to optimize an IoT–based logistics service system under conditions of uncertainty and showed that this approach improves logistics efficiency and accuracy. Azadi et al.44 emphasized that the design of forward supply chain networks (SCNs) should consider customer needs and product innovation. This approach helps identify customer-preferred attributes and allows product pricing to ensure both customer and producer satisfaction. In parallel, studies on CLSCs mainly focus on waste reduction, environmental costs, and uncertainty management. Chouhan et al.45 examined the CLSC of the sugarcane industry. They showed that designing closed-loop networks (CLNs) and managing by-products can reduce waste and improve overall network performance.

Concering the second category of studies, Babaei et al.46, focusing on the design of virtual forward SCNs. They showed that utilizing IoT under uncertainty reduces total costs and enhances the accuracy of network analysis and management processes. Tavana et al.16,17 demonstrated that the utilization of the Artificial Internet of Things (AIoT) in a multi-product CLSC. This models considers the location–inventory–routing problem. It enables the design of a sustainable network with the objective of enhancing network performance and establishing a secure and traceable environment. Saha et al.47 focused on virtual closed-loop supply chains (VCLSCs) and employed technological facilities and metaheuristic algorithms. Their model determined optimal and dynamic investment strategies for managing perishable products.

Overall, under real-world conditions, managing material and information flows in forward and CLSCs can be complex and time-consuming. However, VCLSCs provide an integrated view of the entire process and facilitate information visibility, traceability, and information sharing across SC stakeholders. As a result, they support improved transparency and information management throughout the SC.

In the remainder of this section, and considering the growing attention to these emerging and evolving topics, the literature is examined through four distinct perspectives that include: “Emerging technologies in supply chains”, “Sustainability in supply chains”, “Information security in supply chains” and “Deterministic and stochastic supply chain models”.

Emerging technologies in supply chains

From a technological perspective, the integration of digital technologies, particularly IoT and blockchain, has recently emerged as an important research direction in SC management. In recent years, numerous studies have confirmed the significant role of IoT in enhancing the efficiency of CLSCs. Wang et al.29 demonstrated that the application of IoT in logistics systems improves coordination. It also reduces environmental impacts, and enhances the accuracy of logistical processes in big data-driven environments. Tavana et al.16,17 designed closed-loop supply chain networks (CLSCNs) using IoT technologies. They concluded that these technologies optimize resource utilization, minimize waste, and improve both operational efficiency and network sustainability. Goli et al.36 showed that the use of IoT in multi-product CLSCs enables the design of sustainable and efficient networks. Their model also considers facility location and environmental issues.

Furthermore, in more advanced studies, Tavana et al.16,17 optimized the management of manufacturing, remanufacturing, and distribution of lithium-ion batteries under uncertainty using IoT. This approach increased the operational efficiency of the network. Additionally, Emroozi et al.48 demonstrated that integrating IoT with a multi-criteria group decision-making method in VCLSCs improves the evaluation of raw material quality and environmental impacts. It also enhances the accuracy of network analysis and management processes. Therefore, IoT can provide real-time data acquisition, traceability, and information visibility capabilities that support transparency and information management across SC processes.

Considering the significant role of IoT in real-time data collection and process coordination, Shiri et al.28 optimized forward SC management of the COVID-19 vaccine under uncertainty using blockchain. This approach reduced total costs through enhanced transparency.

Sustainability in supply chains

In recent years, sustainability in CLSCs has received increasing attention. Existing studies suggest that integrating metaheuristic algorithms with advanced technologies can significantly improve the operational efficiency and sustainability of these networks. Zhang et al.18, aimed to mitigate environmental impacts by minimizing carbon dioxide emissions, industrial wastewater, and liquid and solid waste. They demonstrated that a sustainability-oriented approach reduces both setup costs and environmental pollution. Saha et al.47 demonstrated that sustainability-oriented optimization methods improve forecasting accuracy and process control in SCs.

Sheikh Azadi et al.44 employed the Kano model and Game Theory in designing simple and VSCs while considering uncertainty and technological capabilities. Their approach provides a clearer understanding of customer preferences and improves network sustainability and decision-making. Furthermore, Tavana et al.16,17 focused on reducing focused on reducing environmental impacts in lithium-ion battery supply chains. They minimized carbon dioxide emissions and designed a zero-waste CLSC. Their results show that sustainability-oriented design reduces both costs and environmental damage.

Information security in supply chains

Considering the importance of data security, Yadav & Singh49 examined the challenges of information security in the healthcare product SC. They demonstrated that blockchain plays a pivotal role in ensuring the integrity, authenticity, and confidentiality of data in digital logistics. Moreover, the application of machine learning algorithms in real-time data analysis and the extraction of variables related to information credibility enhances the level of data security in supplier selection processes. Overall, the integration of these two technologies leads to a reduction in information vulnerability and an increase in the resilience of the healthcare SC. Therefore, focusing on information security and leveraging advanced technologies can provide an effective approach to ensuring the integrity, authenticity, confidentiality, and resilience of SCs.

Deterministic and stochastic supply chain models

An important perspective in SC modeling relates to how uncertainty is handled within optimization models. In another classification, SC models are divided into deterministic and stochastic categories. Under deterministic conditions, Chouhan et al.45 enabled a precise analysis of costs and environmental impacts in SC problems. They emphasized that decision-making based on deterministic models can enhance SC sustainability. To address uncertainty, Goli et al.36 utilized a robust optimization approach. They showed that multi-product CLNs can adapt to environmental variations and demand fluctuations while reducing costs. Cao et al.50 demonstrated that, under uncertainty, the integrated optimization of location and transportation for medical waste increases economic benefits. It also reduces environmental impacts.

In real markets with continuous demand uncertainty, exclusive reliance on deterministic models may constrain decision-making. In contrast, stochastic models provide greater flexibility and coordination in the flow of goods and information. Accordingly, incorporating uncertainty improves SC performance and enhances the ability to respond to diverse needs.

Literature review.

Problem definition

In this study, a VCLSC equipped with two technologies, IoT and blockchain, is designed. A CLSC incorporates both forward flows of products and reverse flows such as returns, recycling, and remanufacturing. In contrast, VCLSC enhances this structure through the integration of digital technologies. This integration enhances network visibility, traceability, and information availability across the SC. The IoT provides real-time data for product condition monitoring, tracking material flows, and supporting the traceability of defective and returned products. Blockchain ensures transparency, security, and data immutability, enabling reliable information recording and trustworthy traceability of product flows and returned items. In addition to these two technologies, investments are made in the SC to cope with potential threats. The VCLSCN studied in this paper is a multi-echelon, multi-period, multi-product, single-objective CLSC aimed at maximizing SC profit and enhancing sustainability. The forward SC comprises suppliers, manufacturers, distributors, and customers with uncertain demand, while the reverse SC includes disposal, remanufacturing, and collection centers. In the forward flow, raw components are delivered from suppliers to manufacturing centers based on orders. Subsequently, products equipped with IoT-enabled facilities are shipped from manufacturing centers to distribution centers and ultimately to customers. In this SC, two reverse flows are considered. The first reverse flow includes products that reach the end of their life and, based on IoT and blockchain data, are returned from customers to collection centers. In this SC, IoT and blockchain enable tracking of the quantity of reusable products in each period. In the VCLSC, collection centers assign a purchasing cost for returned products to encourage customers return. At the collection centers, products are separated: unusable products are sent to disposal centers, while usable products are sent to remanufacturing centers to be converted into raw components. At remanufacturing centers, specific non-recyclable components in the products are sent to disposal centers. During the second return flow, products damaged during packaging and transit to distribution centers are tracked through IoT and blockchain records and returned to the manufacturing centers. Returned products from the distribution centers are remanufactured or repaired at manufacturing centers, stored there, and shipped in the subsequent period. Additionally, if returned products from distribution centers contain unusable components, they are sent to disposal centers. In this SC, IoT and blockchain facilities are employed at production, distribution, collection, and remanufacturing centers.

To align the model with real SC conditions and reflect market fluctuations, demand in the network is considered uncertain and expressed using fuzzy numbers53. The decision-making horizon in the proposed model includes multiple periods, and the network structure is illustrated in Fig. 1. Fig. 1Proposed virtual closed-loop supply chains network.

Proposed virtual closed-loop supply chains network.

Assumptions

Demand is inherently uncertain and is expressed in fuzzy terms across different periods.A continuous and uninterrupted data flow exists.Products contain recycled components identical to those in new products and sold at the same price.Products manufactured using recycled components are qualitatively comparable to new products and are sold in the market at the same price.Products are returned by customers after a specified period of their lifespan and inspected at the collection center.The inspection cost of each returned item is included in the collection cost.Raw components and products in the forward flow of the VCLSC are stored at manufacturing centers. Products collected from customers in the reverse flow are stored at collection centers.Although IoT does not influence the defect generation process, it enhances system visibility and provides real-time information that supports the tracking of defective products throughout the SC.

Objective function

To maximize the profit of the VCLSC under uncertainty, Eq. (1) models SC costs in two components: normal costs and virtual costs, which are derived from the IoT and blockchain- enabled information. In the proposed VCLSC, two revenue categories are considered: (1) revenue from product sales, and (2) revenue from energy cost savings achieved through IoT.

1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$MaxProfit=\sum_{c}\sum_{e}\sum_{f}\sum_{t}{PRP}_{ct}{Q}_{ceft}+\sum_{t}{R}_{t}^{IoT}-\sum_{t}\left({CF}_{t}+{CE}_{t}+{CS}_{t}+{CR}_{t}+{CI}_{t}+{CB}_{t}+{CQ}_{t}+{CZ}_{t}+{CM}_{t}+{ES}_{t}+{CK}_{t}\right)$$\end{document}

Normal supply chain costs

Equation (2) represents the minimization of the fixed costs in the CLSCN. These costs include fixed ordering costs from suppliers and fixed opening costs of manufacturing, distribution, collection, remanufacturing, and disposal centers.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${CF}_{t}=\sum_{a}{CF}_{at}{IF}_{at}+\sum_{d}{CF}_{d}{IF}_{dt}+\sum_{e}{CF}_{e}{IF}_{et}+\sum_{g}{CF}_{g}{IF}_{gt}+\sum_{h}{CF}_{h}{IF}_{ht}+\sum_{i}{CF}_{i}{IF}_{it} \forall t$$\end{document}

Equation (3) represents the transportation and processing costs in the CLSCN along the forward direction. The first term denotes the purchasing cost of components based on the quantity transferred from suppliers to manufacturing centers. The second term indicates production and processing costs based on the quantity of products produced at manufacturing centers. The third term represents the transportation cost of products from manufacturing centers to distribution centers based on the quantities of produced and remanufactured products. The fourth term denotes the transportation cost of components from suppliers to manufacturing centers based on transferred quantities. The fifth term corresponds to the transportation cost of products from distribution centers to customers.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${CE}_{t}=\sum_{b}\sum_{a}\sum_{d}{CO}_{at}{Q}_{badt}+\sum_{c}\sum_{d}{CP}_{cdt}{Q}_{cdt}+\sum_{c}\sum_{d}\sum_{e}{TR}_{det}\left({Q}_{cdet}+{Q}_{cdet}^{^{\prime}}\right)+\sum_{b}\sum_{a}\sum_{d}{TR}_{adt}{Q}_{badt}+\sum_{c}\sum_{e}\sum_{f}{TR}_{eft}{Q}_{ceft} \forall t$$\end{document}

Equation (4) represents shortage and penalty costs incurred when customer demand is not fulfilled. This cost is calculated on a per-product, per-customer, and per-period basis. Specifically, shortages for each customer in each period are computed, and their total determines the overall shortage cost.4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${CS}_{t}=\sum_{c}\sum_{f}{AP}_{ct}\left({O}_{fct}-{\sum}_{e}{Q}_{ceft}\right) \forall t$$\end{document}

Equation (5) represents the total costs of the CLSC in the reverse flow, which comprises of three sub-equations: (6), (7), and (8).5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${CR}_{t}=\sum_{m=1}^{3}{CR}_{mt} \forall t$$\end{document}

Equation (6) represents the transportation cost of returned products and recycled components. The first term denotes the transportation cost of recycled components from remanufacturing centers to manufacturing centers. The second through seventh terms, respectively, represent the transportation costs of returned products from customers to collection centers, from collection centers to remanufacturing centers, from distribution centers to manufacturing centers, from collection centers to disposal centers, from remanufacturing centers to disposal centers, and from manufacturing centers to disposal centers.

Equation (6) represents the transportation cost of returned products and recycled components. The first term denotes the transportation cost of recycled components from remanufacturing centers to manufacturing centers. The second through seventh terms, respectively, represent the transportation costs of returned products from customers to collection centers, from collection centers to remanufacturing centers, from distribution centers to manufacturing centers, from collection centers to disposal centers, from remanufacturing centers to disposal centers, and from manufacturing centers to disposal centers.

Equation (6) represents the transportation cost of returned products and recycled components. The first term denotes the transportation cost of recycled components from remanufacturing centers to manufacturing centers. The second through seventh terms, respectively, represent the transportation costs of returned products from customers to collection centers, from collection centers to remanufacturing centers, from distribution centers to manufacturing centers, from collection centers to disposal centers, from remanufacturing centers to disposal centers, and from manufacturing centers to disposal centers.

6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \begin{aligned} CR_{{1t}} = & \sum\limits_{b} {\sum\limits_{h} {\sum\limits_{d} {TR_{{hdbt}} } } } Q_{{bhdt}} + \sum\limits_{c} {\sum\limits_{f} {\sum\limits_{g} {TR_{{fgt}} } } } Q_{{cfgt}} + \sum\limits_{c} {\sum\limits_{g} {\sum\limits_{h} {TR_{{ght}} } } } Q_{{cght}} \\ & + \sum\limits_{c} {\sum\limits_{e} {\sum\limits_{d} {Q_{{cdet}} } } } ER_{{cdt}} TR_{{edt}} + \sum\limits_{b} {\sum\limits_{c} {\sum\limits_{g} {\sum\limits_{i} {TR_{{git}} } } } } NPU_{{bct}} Q_{{cgit}} \\ & + \sum\limits_{b} {\sum\limits_{c} {\sum\limits_{h} {\sum\limits_{i} {TR_{{hit}} } } } } NPU_{{bct}} Q_{{chit}} + \sum\limits_{b} {\sum\limits_{c} {\sum\limits_{d} {\sum\limits_{i} {TR_{{dit}} } } } } NPU_{{bct}} Q_{{cdit}} \forall t \\ \end{aligned} $$\end{document}

Equation (7) represents the costs associated with purchasing, collection, classification, and processing of returned products. The first term denotes the total cost of purchasing returned products as well as the collection and classification costs incurred by collection centers for products received from customers. The second term represents the recycling cost of returned products from collection centers at remanufacturing centers. The third term denotes the remanufacturing cost at manufacturing centers for products damaged during the packaging and transportation, which are tracked and recorded through the IoT- and blockchain-enabled infrastructures and returned to the manufacturing centers.

The third term denotes the remanufacturing cost at manufacturing centers for products damaged during the packaging and transportation, which are tracked and recorded through the IoT- and blockchain-enabled infrastructures and returned to the manufacturing centers.

The second term represents the recycling cost of returned products from collection centers at remanufacturing centers. 8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${CR}_{3t}=\sum_{b}\sum_{c}\sum_{i}{TIC}_{bit}{NPU}_{bct}\left(\sum_{d}{Q}_{cdit}+\sum_{h}{Q}_{chit}+\sum_{g}{Q}_{cgit}\right) \forall t$$\end{document}

Equation (9) represents the inventory holding costs of components and products at manufacturing centers, as well as the holding cost of returned products at collection centers. The first term denotes the holding cost based on the final inventory of components at manufacturing centers. These components include raw components purchased from suppliers and components recovered at remanufacturing centers for the production of products. The second term represents the holding cost based on the final inventory of products at manufacturing centers. This inventory includes both manufactured and remanufactured products. The third term denotes the holding cost based on the final inventory of returned products at collection centers.
