Virtual supply chain costs

Equation (10) represents the recall cost of the manufacturing centers in period \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t$$\end{document}, which arises from tracking already defective products using IoT-enabled traceability. Note that, IoT is not modeled as a preventive mechanism that directly affects the defect generation process. Rather, IoT is assumed to be used for post-production traceability and detection of defective products. Therefore, the amount of defective products \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left({FA}_{ct}\right)$$\end{document} is considered an exogenous parameter and is assumed to be independent of IoT integration. This assumption ensures consistency between the mathematical formulation and the role assigned to IoT in the system description.10\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${CM}_{t}=\sum_{c}\sum_{d}{DEF}_{cdt}{Q}_{cdt}{FA}_{ct} \forall t$$\end{document}

Equation (11) represents the fixed cost of facilities related to IoT.11\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${CQ}_{t}=\sum_{d}{CFI}_{d}^{IoT}{IFI}_{dt}+\sum_{e}{CFI}_{e}^{IoT}{IFI}_{et}+\sum_{g}{CFI}_{g}^{IoT}{IFI}_{gt}+\sum_{h}{CFI}_{h}^{IoT}{IFI}_{ht} \forall t$$\end{document}

Equation (12) represents the costs associated with RFID tags. The first term is the cost of purchasing RFID tags for the quantity of products transferred from manufacturing centers to distribution centers. The second term is the cost of remanufacturing RFID tags. This applies to products that are returned from customers to collection centers. These tags are reusable through remanufacturing and only require relabeling. The third term is the cost of purchasing new RFID tags. This applies to products transferred from customers to collection centers when tag reuse is not possible. In this case, newly purchased tags are used.12\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${CZ}_{t}=\sum_{c}\sum_{d}\sum_{e}CT\times {Q}_{cdet}+\sum_{c}\sum_{f}\sum_{g}\mathrm{W} (\mathrm{C}\mathrm{D}\mathrm{H}\times {Q}_{cfgt}-\mathrm{C}\mathrm{T}{\times Q}_{cfg\left(t-LC\right)}) \forall t$$\end{document}

Equation (13) represents the energy consumption cost associated with IoT.13\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${ES}_{t}={ES}_{t}^{IoT}{U}_{t}+{CF}_{t}^{IoT} \forall t$$\end{document}

Equation (14) represents the total data recorded, processed, and transmitted by the IoT in the forward SC and the reverse SC. This occurs at centers equipped with this technology during time period \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t$$\end{document}, which are respectively presented in Eqs. (15) and (16). In Eq. (14), the variable \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${U}_{t}$$\end{document} represents the amount of data collected through the IoT. It reflects the impact of digital monitoring on the system energy consumption. It also denotes the amount of data generated through sensing, monitoring, and communication activities across the SCN. In many IoT-based logistics and manufacturing environments, operational activities such as production, handling, storage, and transportation are monitored by sensors, RFID tags, or smart devices. These technologies periodically generate data associated with product flows. Consistent with this operational logic, the volume of IoT-generated data is considered as a function of the level of product flows in the system. This function, similar to that adopted in the study by Wang et al.54, is assumed to be linear in order to simplify and preserve the solvability of the optimization model for large-scale SCNs. Although, in practice, the level of data generation in IoT systems may also depend on additional factors. This include sensing frequency, communication protocols, device configurations, and system architecture. These factors can create heterogeneous and nonlinear data patterns. However, they are not explicitly modeled in this study. Instead, they are aggregated using a proportional approximation.14\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${U}_{t}= {U}_{t}^{Forward}+{U}_{t}^{Reverse} \forall t$$\end{document}

Equation (15) represents the amount of data recorded, processed, and transmitted by the IoT in the forward SC. This data corresponds to the flow of components and products between facilities. The first term denotes the data transmitted by the IoT in proportion to the quantity of components transferred from suppliers to manufacturing centers. The second term represents IoT data transmission related to manufactured and remanufactured products. These products are transferred from manufacturing centers to distribution centers. The third term represents the data transmitted by the IoT corresponding to the quantity of products transferred from distribution centers to customers.

Equation (15) represents the amount of data recorded, processed, and transmitted by the IoT in the forward SC. This data corresponds to the flow of components and products between facilities. The first term denotes the data transmitted by the IoT in proportion to the quantity of components transferred from suppliers to manufacturing centers. The second term represents IoT data transmission related to manufactured and remanufactured products. These products are transferred from manufacturing centers to distribution centers. The third term represents the data transmitted by the IoT corresponding to the quantity of products transferred from distribution centers to customers.

Equation (15) represents the amount of data recorded, processed, and transmitted by the IoT in the forward SC. This data corresponds to the flow of components and products between facilities. The first term denotes the data transmitted by the IoT in proportion to the quantity of components transferred from suppliers to manufacturing centers. The second term represents IoT data transmission related to manufactured and remanufactured products. These products are transferred from manufacturing centers to distribution centers. The third term represents the data transmitted by the IoT corresponding to the quantity of products transferred from distribution centers to customers.

15\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${U}_{t}^{Forward}=\sum_{b}\sum_{a}\sum_{d}{Q}_{badt}+\sum_{c}\sum_{d}\sum_{e}({Q}_{cdet}+{Q}_{cdet}^{\prime})+\sum_{c}\sum_{e}\sum_{f}{Q}_{ceft} \forall t$$\end{document}

Equation (16) represents the amount of data recorded, processed, and transmitted by the IoT in the reverse SC. This data corresponds to components and products transferred between facilities. The first term denotes the data transmitted by the IoT proportional to the quantity of components transferred from remanufacturing centers to manufacturing centers. The second through fourth terms respectively represent the data transmitted by the IoT corresponding to the quantity of products transferred from distribution centers to manufacturing centers, from customers to collection centers, and from collection centers to remanufacturing centers. The fifth term represents IoT data transmission for products sent to disposal centers. This includes flows from collection centers, remanufacturing centers, and manufacturing centers.

Equation (16) represents the amount of data recorded, processed, and transmitted by the IoT in the reverse SC. This data corresponds to components and products transferred between facilities. The first term denotes the data transmitted by the IoT proportional to the quantity of components transferred from remanufacturing centers to manufacturing centers. The second through fourth terms respectively represent the data transmitted by the IoT corresponding to the quantity of products transferred from distribution centers to manufacturing centers, from customers to collection centers, and from collection centers to remanufacturing centers. The fifth term represents IoT data transmission for products sent to disposal centers. This includes flows from collection centers, remanufacturing centers, and manufacturing centers.

Equation (16) represents the amount of data recorded, processed, and transmitted by the IoT in the reverse SC. This data corresponds to components and products transferred between facilities. The first term denotes the data transmitted by the IoT proportional to the quantity of components transferred from remanufacturing centers to manufacturing centers. The second through fourth terms respectively represent the data transmitted by the IoT corresponding to the quantity of products transferred from distribution centers to manufacturing centers, from customers to collection centers, and from collection centers to remanufacturing centers. The fifth term represents IoT data transmission for products sent to disposal centers. This includes flows from collection centers, remanufacturing centers, and manufacturing centers.

16\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${U}_{t}^{Reverse}=\sum_{b}\sum_{h}\sum_{d}{Q}_{bhdt}+\sum_{c}\sum_{e}\sum_{d}{Q}_{cedt}+\sum_{c}\sum_{f}\sum_{g}{Q}_{cfgt}+\sum_{c}\sum_{g}\sum_{h}{Q}_{cght}+\sum_{c}\sum_{i}\left(\sum_{g}{Q}_{cgit}+\sum_{h}{Q}_{chit}+\sum_{d}{Q}_{cdit}\right) \forall t$$\end{document}

Equation (17) specifies the fixed IoT energy cost when IoT is utilized in any of the centers.17\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${CF}_{t}^{IoT}=\sum_{d}{CF}_{dt}^{IoT}{IFI}_{dt}+\sum_{e}{CF}_{et}^{IoT}{IFI}_{et}+\sum_{g}{CF}_{gt}^{IoT}{IFI}_{gt}+\sum_{h}{CF}_{ht}^{IoT}{IFI}_{ht} \forall t$$\end{document}

Equation (18) represents the information security cost for data collected through the IoT enablers. The probability of various threats is represented by the set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z= \left\{{{{\mathrm{z}}_{1t},\mathrm{z}}_{2t},\dots ,\mathrm{z}}_{\mathrm{j}\mathrm{t}}\right\}$$\end{document}. Implementing a security system for threat \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j$$\end{document} at time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t$$\end{document} requires a total investment value \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${AH}_{jt}$$\end{document}.18\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${CK}_{t}={DQ}_{jt}{Z}_{jt}+{AH}_{jt} \forall j,t$$\end{document}

Equation (19) represents the fixed cost of blockchain implementation at different centers.19\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${CB}_{t}=\sum_{d}{C}_{d}^{Bc}{IFB}_{dt}+\sum_{e}{C}_{e}^{Bc}{IFB}_{et}+\sum_{g}{C}_{g}^{Bc}{IFB}_{gt}+\sum_{h}{C}_{h}^{Bc}{IFB}_{ht} \forall t$$\end{document}

Equation (20) represents the energy cost savings of IoT in centers equipped with IoT devices. This effect is incorporated as an exogenous economic benefit associated with IoT adoption and does not alter the operational decision structure of the optimization model.20\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${R}_{t}^{IoT}=\sum_{d}{R}_{dt}^{IoT}{IFI}_{dt}+\sum_{e}{R}_{et}^{IoT}{IFI}_{et}+\sum_{g}{R}_{gt}^{IoT}{IFI}_{gt}+\sum_{h}{R}_{ht}^{IoT}{IFI}_{ht} \forall t$$\end{document}

Normal supply chain constraints

In the SC under study, the quantity of returned products is determined by the quantity of products sold by distribution centers in previous periods. It is assumed that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${e}_{cft}$$\end{document} represents the number of used products available in period \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t$$\end{document}. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${E}_{cft}=\left\{1,\dots ,{e}_{cft}\right\}$$\end{document} denotes the set of used products whose useful life has expired. For reusable returned products, data are stored in a database based on IoT and blockchain enabler. Constraints (21–24) determine the quantity of returned used products. When \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\le LC$$\end{document}, no used products whose expiration date has passed are available. Therefore, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${e}_{cft}=0 and {E}_{cft}=\varnothing $$\end{document}. If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t>LC$$\end{document}, the products purchased in period \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t-LC$$\end{document} reach the end of their lifetime. These products can then be returned by customers to the SC. The quantity of returned products is obtained by multiplying the actual return rate by the quantity of recyclable returned products. In the proposed model, the level of returned used products is always nonnegative. Therefore, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${RET}_{ct} \ge 0$$\end{document} is imposed. According to Constraints (21), the relationship between the buying price of returned products and the scaling coefficient is modeled as an exponential function. In this function, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta $$\end{document} denotes the expected reward value for customers.21\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${N}_{t}=\sum_{c}\sum_{g}{e}^{- \frac{{TB}_{cgt}}{\theta }} \forall t$$\end{document}22\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${RET}_{ct}=\left(1-{N}_{t}\right){MR}_{ct} \forall c, t$$\end{document}23\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${E}_{cft}=\sum_{e}{Q}_{cef\left(t-LC\right)} \forall c,f,\forall t>LC$$\end{document}24\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum_{g}{Q}_{cfgt}={RET}_{ct}{E}_{cft} \forall c,f,\forall t>LC$$\end{document}

Constraint (25) represents the quantity of final inventory of components in each time period at manufacturing centers. The first term is the safety stock of components at the manufacturing centers. The second term is the quantity of final inventory of components at the manufacturing centers in the previous period. The third term is the quantity of components transferred from suppliers to the manufacturing centers. The fourth term is the quantity of components transferred from remanufacturing centers to the manufacturing centers. The fifth term represents components consumed in products sent from manufacturing centers to disposal centers. The sixth term represents used components contained in manufactured and remanufactured products from the previous period. The fifth and sixth terms reduce the inventory level of components at manufacturing centers. This is because these components are consumed within SC processes.25\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${NOI}_{bdt}={SP}_{bdt}+{NOI}_{bd\left(t-1\right)}+\sum_{a}{Q}_{badt}+\sum_{h}{Q}_{bhdt}-\sum_{c}\sum_{i}{NPU}_{bct}{Q}_{cdit}-\sum_{c}\sum_{e}{NPU}_{bct}\left(1-{FA}_{ct}\right)\left({Q}_{cdet}+{Q}_{cde\left(t-1\right)}{\prime}\right) \forall b,d,t$$\end{document}

Constraint (26) represents the final inventory level of products in each time period at manufacturing centers. The first term is the final inventory level of products at manufacturing centers in the previous period. The second term is the quantity of products manufactured at the manufacturing centers. The third term is the quantity of remanufactured products that were sent from manufacturing centers to distribution centers in the previous period. The fourth term also represents the total quantity of manufactured and remanufactured products transferred from manufacturing centers to distribution centers in the previous period. This outflow reduces the inventory level at manufacturing centers due to product shipments.26\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${NOI}_{cdt}={NOI}_{cd(t-1)}+{Q}_{cdt}+\sum_{e}{Q}_{cde(t-1)}{ER}_{cdt}-\sum_{e}{(Q}_{cdet}+{Q}_{cde\left(t-1\right)}{\prime}) \forall c,d,t$$\end{document}

Constraint (27) specifies the final inventory level of returned products from customers at collection centers in each period. The first term is the final inventory level of products at the collection centers in the previous period. The second term is the quantity of products returned from customers to the collection centers. The third term is the quantity of products transferred from the collection centers to recycling, and the fourth term is the quantity of products transferred from the collection centers to disposal centers. The third and fourth terms reduce the inventory level of the collection centers because products leave the collection centers through recycling and disposal processes.27\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${NOI}_{cgt}={NOI}_{cg(t-1)}+\sum_{f}{Q}_{cfgt}-\sum_{h}{Q}_{cght}-\sum_{i}{Q}_{cgit} \forall c,g,\forall t>LC$$\end{document}

Constraints (28) and (29) represent the quantities of products transferred to remanufacturing centers and disposal centers, respectively.28\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum_{h}{Q}_{cght}=\sum_{f}{RP}_{ct}{Q}_{cfgt} \forall c,g,t$$\end{document}29\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum_{i}{Q}_{cgit}=\sum_{f}\left(1-{RP}_{ct}\right){Q}_{cfgt} \forall c,g,t$$\end{document}

Constraint (30) represents the quantity of recycled components. Constraint (31), states that if the product lifetime is less than or equal to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$LC$$\end{document}, no products are sent to remanufacturing centers. Consequently, no recycled components exist. Constraint (32) represents the quantity of products transferred from remanufacturing centers to disposal centers.30\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum_{c}\sum_{g}\left(\left(1-{DHI}_{cht}\right){NPU}_{bct}{Q}_{cght}\right)=\sum_{d}{Q}_{bhdt} \forall b,h,\forall t>LC$$\end{document}31\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${Q}_{bhdt}=0 \forall b,h,d,\forall t\le LC$$\end{document}32\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum_{g}{DHI}_{cht}{Q}_{cght}=\sum_{i}{Q}_{chit} \forall c,h,t$$\end{document}

Constraint (33) determines the total quantity of components purchased from suppliers and transferred from remanufacturing centers. This quantity is based on the number of components consumed in producing new and remanufactured products at the manufacturing centers. Constraint (34) assumes that the production quantity at the manufacturing centers is limited by customer demand. If the production level falls below the required demand, the resulting shortage is included in the objective function as a penalty cost. Constraint (35) specifies the quantity of defective products transferred from manufacturing centers to disposal centers. Constraint (36) relates to the quantity of non-defective products produced at the manufacturing centers. Constraint (37) relates to the quantity of non-defective products shipped from the distribution centers to customers. Constraint (38) specifies that the quantity of returned products in period t must be less than or equal the quantity of products allocated to customers in period \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t-LC$$\end{document}. Constraints (39–44) ensure that the quantity of products does not exceed the capacity of the centers.33\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum_{c}\sum_{e}{NPU}_{bct}\left({Q}_{cdet}+{Q}_{cdet}^{^{\prime}}\right)=\sum_{a}{Q}_{badt}+\sum_{h}{Q}_{bhdt} \forall b,d,t$$\end{document}34\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum_{d}{Q}_{cdt}\le \sum_{f}{O}_{fct} \forall c,t$$\end{document}35\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum_{e}{FA}_{ct}\left({Q}_{cdet}+{Q}_{cdet}^{^{\prime}}\right)=\sum_{i}{Q}_{cdit} \forall c,d,t$$\end{document}36\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${Q}_{cdt}=\left(1-{FA}_{ct}\right)\left(\sum_{e}{Q}_{cdet}+\sum_{e}{Q}_{cdet}^{^{\prime}}\right) \forall c,d,t$$\end{document}37\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum_{d}(1-{ER}_{cdt}){Q}_{cdet}^{^{\prime}}+\sum_{d}(1-{ER}_{cdt}){Q}_{cdet}=\sum_{f}{Q}_{ceft} \forall c,e,t$$\end{document}38\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum_{e}{Q}_{cef(t-LC)}\ge \sum_{g}{Q}_{cfgt} \forall c,f,\forall t>LC$$\end{document}39\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum_{b}{Q}_{badt}\le {CAP}_{at}{IF}_{at} \forall a,d,t$$\end{document}40\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum_{c}{Q}_{cdt}\le {CAP}_{dt}{IF}_{dt} \forall d,t$$\end{document}41\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum_{c}{Q}_{cdet}+\sum_{c}{Q}_{cdet}^{^{\prime}}\le {CAP}_{et}{IF}_{et} \forall d,e,t$$\end{document}42\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum_{f}{Q}_{cfgt}\le {CAP}_{gt}{IF}_{gt} \forall c,g,t$$\end{document}43\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum_{g}{Q}_{cght}\le {CAP}_{ht}{IF}_{ht} \forall c,h,t$$\end{document}44\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum_{g}{Q}_{cgit}+\sum_{h}{Q}_{chit}+\sum_{d}{Q}_{cdit}\le {CAP}_{it}{IF}_{it} \forall c,i,t$$\end{document}

Constraint (45) ensures that at least one center \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\in \left\{d,e,g,h,i\right\}$$\end{document} exists in the VCLSCN at any time. It also ensures that products collected from customers are returned to a collection center. If no returned products are available, collection and remanufacturing centers are not opened. Constraint (46) restricts the final decision regarding the operation of an opened center. If the model decides to open a center at a specific time, the center remains active thereafter. Constraint (47) ensures the non-negativity of the decision variables. Constraint (48) ensures the binary nature of the decision variables related to center opening.45\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum_{k}{IF}_{kt}\ge 1 \forall t$$\end{document}46\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${IF}_{k\left(t+1\right)}-{IF}_{kt}\ge 0 \forall t,\forall k\in \left\{d,e,g,h,i\right\}$$\end{document}\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${Q}_{badt},{Q}_{cdt},{Q}_{cdet},{Q}_{cdet}^{^{\prime}},{Q}_{ceft},{Q}_{cfgt},{Q}_{cght},{Q}_{cgit},{Q}_{bhdt}, {Q}_{chit},{Q}_{cdit}{,Q}_{cedt}, {NOI}_{bdt}{,NOI}_{cdt}$$\end{document}47\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${NOI}_{cgt},{E}_{cft}\ge 0 \forall b,c,a,d,e,f,g,h,i,t$$\end{document}48\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${IF}_{at}{,IF}_{dt},{IF}_{et},{IF}_{gt},{IF}_{ht}{,IF}_{it}\in \left\{\mathrm{0,1}\right\} \forall a,d,e,g,h,i,t$$\end{document}

Virtual supply chain constraints

Constraint (49) indicates that the total cost of securing information collected through IoT should not exceed the budget allocated to the overall security of the SC. Constraint (50) represents different investment scenarios for mitigating threats, where increasing investment leads to a reduction in the probability of threats.49\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${CK}_{t}\le S \forall t$$\end{document}50\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${AH}_{jt}=\left\{\begin{array}{c}2000 0.6\le {Z}_{jt}\le 0.8\\ 4000 0.4\le {Z}_{jt}\le 0.6\\ 6000 0.1\le {Z}_{jt}\le 0.4\end{array}\right. \forall j,t$$\end{document}

Constraints (51–54) ensure that IoT facilities are not used for centers that have not been opened.51\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${IFI}_{dt}\le {IF}_{dt} \forall d,t$$\end{document}52\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${IFI}_{et}\le {IF}_{et} \forall e,t$$\end{document}53\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${IFI}_{gt}\le {IF}_{gt} \forall g,t$$\end{document}54\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${IFI}_{ht}\le {IF}_{ht} \forall h,t$$\end{document}

Constraints (55–58) describe the forward and reverse SC processes. Constraints (55) and (56) ensure capacity limitations for product flows in facilities where blockchain-enabled traceability is implemented. These constraints ensure that product flows from manufacturing centers to distribution centers do not exceed maximum capacity. They also ensure that product flows from distribution centers to customers remain within capacity limits. In the reverse SC, Constraints (57) and (58) enforce similar capacity for facilities equipped with blockchain-enabled traceability infrastructure. Constraint (57) limits the flow from collection centers to remanufacturing centers. Constraint (58) limits the flow from remanufacturing centers to disposal centers. These constraints ensure that all transfers remain within the maximum capacity of the corresponding centers.55\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum_{c}{(Q}_{cdet}+{Q}_{cdet}^{^{\prime}})\le {CAP}_{dt}{IFB}_{dt} \forall d,e,t$$\end{document}56\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum_{f}{Q}_{ceft}\le {CAP}_{et}{IFB}_{et} \forall c,e,t$$\end{document}57\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum_{h}{Q}_{cght}\le {CAP}_{gt}{IFB}_{gt} \forall c,g,t$$\end{document}58\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum_{i}{Q}_{chit}\le {CAP}_{ht}{IFB}_{ht} \forall c,h,t$$\end{document}

Constraint (59) ensures the binary nature of the decision variables related to IoT and blockchain usage. Constraint (60) specifies the non-negativity of the decision variables for the amount of recorded, processed, and transmitted data, as well as the fixed IoT energy cost. Constraint (61) states that if blockchain is implemented at a facility in a given period, it remains active in the network from that period onward. This technology continues to be utilized in subsequent periods without interruption.59\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${IFI}_{dt},{IFI}_{et},{IFI}_{gt},{IFI}_{ht},{IFB}_{dt},{IFB}_{et},{IFB}_{ht},{IFB}_{gt}\in \left\{0,1\right\} \forall d,e,g,h,t$$\end{document}60\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${U}_{t},{CF}_{t}^{IoT}\ge 0 \forall t$$\end{document}61\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${IFB}_{k\left(t+1\right)}-{IFB}_{kt}\ge 0 \forall t,\forall k\in \left\{d,e,g,h,i\right\}$$\end{document}

Uncertainty management

To account for uncertainty in the demand parameter, it is assumed that the parameter follows a triangular fuzzy structure. In this context, the most probable value of the demand \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$({O}_{fct})$$\end{document} is determined using a random number generated from a predefined distribution. The most optimistic value (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${O}_{fct}^{o})$$\end{document} and most pessimistic value \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${(O}_{fct}^{p})$$\end{document} are calculated according to Eqs. (62) and (63).62\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${O}_{fct}^{o}=\left(1+r\right){O}_{fct}$$\end{document}63\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${O}_{fct}^{p}=\left(1-r\right){O}_{fct}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r$$\end{document} is a random number following a continuous uniform distribution in the interval \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left[0, 1\right]$$\end{document}. By employing the Jiménez55 method to handel fuzzy uncertainty, Eqs. (4) and (34) are rewritten as Eqs. (64) and (65), respectively.64\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${CS}_{t}= \sum_{c}\sum_{f}{AP}_{ct}(\left(\frac{{O}_{fct}^{p}+2{O}_{fct}+{O}_{fct}^{o}}{4}\right)-\sum_{e}{Q}_{ceft}) \forall t$$\end{document}65\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum_{d}{Q}_{cdt}\le \alpha \left(\frac{{O}_{fct}+{O}_{fct}^{o}}{2}\right)+\left(1-\alpha \right)\left(\frac{{O}_{fct}^{p}+{O}_{fct}}{2}\right) \forall c,f,t$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha $$\end{document} is set to 0.5 to represent a moderate level of uncertainty.

Numerical example

Since the model is NP-hard, the GWO algorithm is employed to solve large-scale problems. To obtain the optimal parameter values of the enhanced GWO algorithm, the Taguchi method is utilized. Moreover, the reliability and validity of the proposed solution method are evaluated through a comparison of the results obtained from the GAMS software and those obtained from the GWO algorithm. This comparison is conducted for small-, medium-, and large-scale problem instances. The proposed model is implemented for large-scale problems using MATLAB R2024b. The results of the metaheuristic algorithm are then evaluated and compared under various conditions.

Parameter values used in the developed model

For generating numerical examples, parameter values are randomly generated according to the distributions provided in in Table 3. The parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta $$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$LC$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S$$\end{document} are excluded from this process. The numerical data used in this study are designed to represent a CLSC in the electronic components sector. This includes returned small electronic devices such as mobile phones and their accessories. Therefore, the assumed ranges for the uncertain parameters are not selected arbitrarily. Rather, they are determined based on realistic industrial assumptions associated with electronic product recovery systems. Subsequently, for each uncertain parameter, except for product demand, a value is randomly generated from the corresponding range and utilized in solving the deterministic optimization model. As noted in the uncertainty management section, the demand parameter is uncertain. Therefore, it is determined using Eqs. (64) and (65). Optimization and solution method

In this study, the solution space grows exponentially as the number of variables increases. This characteristic classifies the model as an NP-hard problem. To address this challenge, metaheuristic algorithms are suggested for solving the proposed model at a large scale. In this context, the GWO algorithm is employed which originally was proposed by Mirjalili et al.56. It is a nature-inspired metaheuristic algorithm that simulates the leadership hierarchy and hunting behavior of grey wolves. In this algorithm, candidate solutions are categorized into four groups, namely alpha (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha $$\end{document}), beta (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta $$\end{document}), delta (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta $$\end{document}), and omega (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega $$\end{document}). The alpha, beta and delta wolves represent the best three solutions, respectively, while the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega $$\end{document} wolves denote the remaining search agents. The search process is guided by the first to third best wolves (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha , \beta , \delta $$\end{document}), which direct the movement of the remaining search agents toward promising regions of the solution space. Although many metaheuristic algorithms exist, empirical evidence and previous studies indicate the competitive performance of the GWO algorithm in SC optimization57,58.

The main steps of the GWO algorithm implemented in this study are summarized as follows:

Step 1: Initialization

Step 1: Initialization

Step 1: Initialization

Generate an initial population of wolves within the predefined bounds of the decision variables.

Step 2: Fitness Evaluation

Step 2: Fitness Evaluation

Step 2: Fitness Evaluation

Evaluate the objective (fitness) function for each search agent.

Step 3: Leadership Identification

Step 3: Leadership Identification

Step 3: Leadership Identification

Identify the best three solutions as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha $$\end{document} (best), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta $$\end{document} (second best), and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta $$\end{document} (third best).

Step 4: Parameter Update

Step 4: Parameter Update

Step 4: Parameter Update

Update the control parameter (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a$$\end{document}), which decreases linearly from 2 to 0 during iterations to to ensure a proper balance between exploration and exploitation.

Step 5: Position Update

Step 5: Position Update

Step 5: Position Update

Update the position of each wolf based on the positions of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha , \beta ,$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta $$\end{document} using coefficient vectors (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A$$\end{document}) and (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C$$\end{document}), where random vectors (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${r}_{1}$$\end{document}) and (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${r}_{2}$$\end{document}) are drawn from a uniform distribution in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[0, 1].$$\end{document}

Step 6: Termination

Step 6: Termination

Step 6: Termination

Repeat Steps 2–5 until the maximum number of iterations (MaxIt) is reached.

A more detailed explanation of these steps is provided through the corresponding pseudo-code in Fig. 2.Fig. 2Pseudo-code Grey Wolf Optimizer.

Pseudo-code Grey Wolf Optimizer.

Before conducting numerical analyses with the developed GWO algorithm, its initial parameters must be optimized. In this study, the Taguchi method is used for parameter tuning59. The Taguchi method is a statistical approach for parameter design that uses orthogonal arrays and signal-to-noise (S/N) ratios to identify optimal parameter settings with minimal experimental runs. Based on the results in Table 5, the optimal population size (Npop) is set to 75 wolves and the maximum number of iterations (MaxIt) is fixed at 300. To mitigate the stochastic behavior of the algorithm, each computational experiment is repeated 3 independent runs. The best objective function value, obtained from the three repetitions, is recorded as the final result. According to the results, the mean signal-to-noise (S/N) ratio plot of the GWO algorithm is shown in Fig. 3. This plot indicates that when Maxit is set at level 3 and Npop at level 2, the algorithm achieves its best performance in searching for near-optimal solutions.Table 5General and algorithm-specific General and algorithm-specific parameters of the Grey Wolf Optimizer used in this study.

Mean signal-to-noise ratio for optimization the two key parameters, Maxit and Npop, at three different levels and determination of their optimal levels.

Moreover, the random number generator is initialized with a fixed seed to ensure reproducibility of the results. The decision variables are initialized using a uniform random distribution within the search bounds \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[0, 1]$$\end{document}, and the algorithm is terminated upon reaching the maximum number of iterations. In the GWO search mechanism, the control parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a$$\end{document} decreases linearly from 2 to 0 throughout the iterations to balance exploration and exploitation. The random coefficients \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${r}_{1}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${r}_{2}$$\end{document}, generated in the interval \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[0, 1]$$\end{document}, are used to compute the coefficient vectors \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A$$\end{document} and C. The search process is guided by the three leading wolves \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Alpha$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Beta$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Delta$$\end{document}, and the position of each wolf is updated according to the average position of these leaders.

Subsequently, the validation of the developed GWO algorithm is examined. In this regard, eight problems over 12 time periods were generated. These problems range from small to large dimensions. They are solved using the CPLEX solver in GAMS and the GWO metaheuristic algorithm. The problem specifications are presented in Table 6. The results related to objective function values and solution timses are reported in Table 7. Problems 1–6 are successfully solved in GAMS. The time limit is 1800 s with a maximum of 6 iterations. However, Problems 7 and 8 can not be solved due to their larger dimensions and the time constraint. This result highlights the limitation of exact methods for NP-hard problems. To address this challenge, the problems are also solved using the GWO algorithm. For Problems 1–6, the algorithm’s results are very close to those obtained by the CPLEX solver, while the solution time is significantly lower. This results indicate the computational efficiency and scalability of the algorithm. For this purpose, the model is solved in GAMS, and the GWO algorithm is executed in MATLAB R2024b. A system with a Core i5 processor and 16 GB of RAM is used.Table 6Sizes of numerical examples at different scales.Sample problemFCEGHIBDAJ18224444233210244444443312365556654414385556864516410777810746184127778128573652414141416241658405281616162028205Table 7Profit values and solution times of various numerical examples in Grey Wolf Optimizer and GAMS: across different problem dimensions.Sample problemGAMSGWO1ProfitTime in secondsProfitTime in seconds111,799,958111,793,9800.819261215,878,844215,401,6901.253696337,866,446637,815,4505.8941828450,344,0822150,154,79010.904495128,037,055142128,008,10040.870466171,336,6461469171,111,600158.15197Memory limit–399,214,385.2368.49398Memory limit–540,531,307.4659.60412931GWO: Grey Wolf Optimizer.

Comparative studies

The aim of this section is to evaluate the impact of integrating IoT and blockchain technologies in the SC. Two comparative studies are conducted for this purpose. In the first study, the performance of the developed model is compared with a similar model that does not incorporate IoT and blockchain technologies. In the second study, the effect of blockchain technology is examined by comparing the developed model with a similar model without blockchain. The analyses are conducted using test problem 8, which represents a large-scale problem. For the purpose of these evaluations, all cost, revenue, and profit values in Figs. 3, 4, 5, 6, 7 and 8 and Tables 8, 9, 10 and 11 are expressed in monetary units (MU), and the planning horizon consists of 12 time periods.Fig. 4Comparison of Supply chain profit over 12 time periods for the proposed model and the model without Internet of Things and Blockchain.Fig. 5Total supply chain costs over 12 time periods for the proposed model and the model without Internet of Things and Blockchain.Fig. 6Supply chain revenue over 12 time periods for the proposed model and the model without Internet of Things and Blockchain.Fig. 7Comparison of supply chain profit over 12 time periods for the model with Internet of Things only and the proposed model.Fig. 8Total supply chain costs over 12 time periods for the model with Internet of Things only and the proposed model.Table 8Supply chain profit over 12 time periods for the proposed model and the model without Internet of Things and Blockchain.PeriodWith IoT1 & BlockchainWithout IoT & BlockchainT119,861,240.744,946,414.983T223,759,532.737,331,783.932T335,695,875.721,529,149.07T438,809,719.723,369,894.08T557,181,420.2533,317,991.86T652,835,423.9631,183,383.87T756,989,20734,918,442.17T858,651,722.2334,147,449.95T964,391,250.9836,866,857.49T1057,239,362.1233,285,567.39T1150,603,039.0528,314,672.89T1224,513,512.946,706,002.4641IOT: Internet of Things

Comparison of the proposed model with a similar model without Internet of Things and Blockchain technologies

The comparison of profits obtained from the proposed model and a similar model without IoT and blockchain technologies over 12 periods is shown in Fig. 4 and Table 8. In this comparison, the profit of each model is evaluated based on the total costs and revenues of the SC. The corresponding costs and revenues results are illustrated in Fig. 5 and 6, respectively. In addition, the percentage of customer demand fulfillment over 12 time periods is reported in Table 9.

A comparison of the decision variable values in the two proposed models and the similar model without technology indicates that in most of the 12 time periods. The implementation of IoT and blockchain increases the number of opened manufacturing and distribution centers from 1 to 3, while the number of opened collection, remanufacturing, and disposal centers changes from 1 to 5. Consequently, the facility opening cost increases by 23.33%; whereas during periods 1–9, which exhibit an increasing demand trend, the average product flow in the forward SC increases. Specifically, the quantity of components purchased from suppliers, increases from 0.05 to 0.2, and the quantity of manufactured and remanufactured products sent to distribution centers increases from 0.23 to 0.78. In addition, the quantity of products sent to customers increases from 0.44 to 0.64. As a result, the forward SC cost increases by 44.67%, while sales revenue increases by 63.32%. It is noteworthy that, upon implementation of the IoT and blockchain, the total system revenue from energy saving increases by 34.2%. However, the deployment of these technologies also introduces additional costs. These include the fixed cost of IoT facilities and the fixed cost of blockchain facilities. These costs increase the total system cost by 1.07% and 2.01%, respectively.

The opening of a greater number of manufacturing, collection, remanufacturing, and disposal centers under IoT and blockchain implementation leads to similar changes in the reverse flow segment. Accordingly, the quantity of returned products transferred to collection centers increases from 0.10 to 0.60, and the quantity of products transferred to remanufacturing centers increases from 0.41 to 0.71. This results in a larger share of returned products entering recycling and remanufacturing process, while the quantity of products sent to disposal centers decreases. Specifically, the average disposal quantity decreases from 0.63 to 0.27 in collection centers, from 0.52 to 0.17 in remanufacturing centers, and from 0.41 to 0.18 in manufacturing centers. Therefore, the traceability and information-management capabilities enabled by IoT and blockchain support more effective management of product returns, recycling, and remanufacturing activities. The computational results indicate a 73% reduction in reverse logistics cost under the proposed model. With the increase in forward and reverse activities, inventory levels also increase. The average final inventory of materials in manufacturing centers rises from 0.02 to 0.41. The final inventory of products in manufacturing centers rises from 0.05 to 0.56. The final inventory of products in collection centers rises from 0.04 to 0.38. The outcome of these changes is a 68.66% increase in inventory holding cost and a 25.3% decrease in shortage cost. Ultimately, with the implementation of IoT and blockchain, total SC profit increases by 79.33%, and the demand fulfillment rate over the twelve periods improves by 4.37%.

Since in periods 10–12, a decreasing trend in demand is observed, the implementation of IoT and blockchain leads to a different pattern in profit and demand fulfillment rate. In these periods, the average quantity of production and distribution decreases from 0.78 to 0.49. The quantity of products sent to customers decreases from 0.73 to 0.66. The quantity of products transferred to collection centers also decreases from 0.60 to 0.52. In addition, the quantity of products transferred to remanufacturing centers decreases from 0.71 to 0.64 in the proposed model compared to the model without technology. Under the proposed model, the quantity of recycled components decreases slightly from 0.72 to 0.69. At the same time, visibility and traceability of returned products are improved through IoT and blockchain integration. This indicates that even with demand reduction, the share of recycled materials in supplying raw materials for production remains significant. In contrast, the quantity of products sent to disposal centers increases slightly during this period. This increase occurs because some returned products are not economically feasible for remanufacturing due to reduced demand. For this reason, the quantity of products transferred to disposal centers increases from 0.27 to 0.31 in collection centers, from 0.17 to 0.21 in remanufacturing centers, and from 0.18 to 0.22 in manufacturing centers. However, the inventory level of components in manufacturing centers decreases from 0.41 to 0.37, the inventory level of products in manufacturing centers decreases from 0.56 to 0.49, and the inventory level of products in collection centers decreases from 0.38 to 0.33. In these periods, sales revenue under IoT and blockchain implementation improves by 58%, and total revenue from energy saving improves by 26.21%. In addition, the fixed cost of facility opening, forward SC cost, and inventory holding cost increase by 21%, 34%, and 54%, respectively, while reverse logistics cost decreases by 65%. It is noteworthy that technology implementation cost constitutes 20.56% of the total cost, and under these conditions, shortage cost decreases by 18%. In summary, profit increases by 68% and the demand fulfillment rate increases by 3.83% compared to the model without technology.

A comparison of the trend of changes indicates that the importance of utilizing IoT and blockchain varies across different periods. The most significant effect is observed during the middle periods of the demand cycle, specifically in period’s six to nine. During these periods, the volume of demand and returned products reaches its maximum level. Therefore, simultaneous management of forward and reverse flows becomes more complex. Under these conditions, the effects of digital technologies on network operations become more visible. These technologies enhance product traceability and provide more reliable information for network operations. They also provide informational support for managing recycling and remanufacturing processes. In addition, improved traceability and information availability provide decision-makers with better information for managing product flows and disposal decisions. As a result, the solutions obtained under the proposed model exhibit higher profit and demand-fulfillment rates during these periods. In contrast, during the first and second periods due to limited operational volume, and during periods ten to twelve due to demand reduction, the effect of utilizing these technologies is less compared to the middle periods. Nevertheless, even during these periods, digital infrastructures provide visibility and information support that facilitate network coordination and help mitigate performance deterioration across the network.

Comparison of the proposed model with a similar model not equipped with Blockchain technology

The comparison of profit over 12 periods between the proposed model (with IoT and blockchain) and a similar model equipped with IoT only (without blockchain capability) is presented in Fig. 7 and Table 10. In this analysis, the profit of each model is examined based on the total costs and revenues of the SC, and the results are shown in Fig. 8 and 9, respectively. In addition, the percentage of customer demand fulfillment over 12 time periods is presented in Table 11.Table 10Supply chain profit over 12 time periods for the model with Internet of Things only and the proposed model.PeriodWith IoT1 & BlockchainWithout BlockchainT119,861,240.745,844,777.846T223,759,532.738,349,719.381T335,695,875.723,108,368.71T438,809,719.725,394,122.62T557,181,420.2535,219,881.72T652,835,423.9633,695,445.29T756,989,20737,841,636.24T858,651,722.2337,523,318.38T964,391,250.9841,458,582.7T1057,239,362.1236,920,508.39T1150,603,039.0532,889,273.47T1224,513,512.9410,146,482.571IOT: Internet of ThingsFig. 9Supply chain revenue over 12 time periods for the model with Internet of Things only and the proposed model.Table 11Percentage of customer demand fulfillment over 12 time periods for the model with Internet of Things only the proposed model.PeriodWith IoT1 & BlockchainWithout BlockchainT10.8940.791T20.9090.905T30.9100.909T40.9210.913T50.9200.910T60.8970.888T70.9100.903T80.9020.891T90.9230.918T100.9190.912T110.9150.907T120.9040.900
