The results obtained from the proposed models and the similar model without blockchain indicate that, in most of the 12 time periods, the implementation of blockchain increases the number of opened manufacturing, distribution, collection, remanufacturing, and disposal centers from 3 to 5. As a result of this change, on the one hand, the facility opening cost increases by 18.5%. On the other hand, during periods 1–9, demand shows an increasing trend. In these periods, the activation of blockchain increases products flows in both forward and reverse SCs. The forward SC flow includes the quantity of components purchased from suppliers. This flow increases on average from 0.07 to 0.20. The quantity of manufactured and remanufactured products sent to distribution centers increases from 0.37 to 0.78. Moreover, the products delivered to customers increase from 0.48 to 0.64. This results in a 37% increase in forward SC cost. It also leads to a 55.33% increase in sales revenue. It is noteworthy that blockchain implementation cost accounts for 0.27% of the total cost. At the same time, energy-saving revenue in the proposed model is 20% higher than that of the IoT-only model. Additionally, the costs related to the recall operations and IoT deployment in the proposed model do not exhibit a significant change compared to the model equipped solely with IoT.

The opening of a greater number of manufacturing, collection, remanufacturing, and disposal centers with blockchain implementation is associated similar changes in the reverse flow segment. Accordingly, the average quantity of returned products transferred to collection centers also increases from 0.35 to 0.60. The quantity of returned products transferred to remanufacturing centers also increases from 0.53 to 0.71. Under the proposed model, blockchain-enabled traceability is associated with additional information visibility regarding returned products across the network. The computational results show that the average disposal quantity decreases from 0.63 to 0.53 in collection centers. It decreases from 0.52 to 0.47 in remanufacturing centers. It also decreases from 0.41 to 0.32 in manufacturing centers. The computational results indicate a 63% lower reverse logistics cost in the proposed model compared with the IoT-only model. With the increase in forward and reverse flows, the average final inventory of components in manufacturing centers increases from 0.27 to 0.41. The final inventory of products in manufacturing centers increases from 0.33 to 0.56. The final inventory of products in collection centers increases from 0.21 to 0.38. The aforementioned changes lead to a 20.2% reduction in shortage cost and a 44% increase in inventory holding cost. Ultimately, with blockchain implementation, the total SC profit increases by 64.29%. In addition, the demand fulfillment rate across the twelve periods improves by 1.99%.

Due to the reduction in demand during periods 10–12, contrary to previous periods, blockchain implementation leads to a different pattern in profit and demand fulfillment rate. In these periods, the average quantity of production and distribution decreases from 0.78 to 0.56. The quantity of products delivered to customers decreases from 0.73 to 0.69. The quantity of products transferred to collection centers also decreases from 0.60 to 0.54. In addition, products transferred to remanufacturing centers decrease from 0.71 to 0.67. The quantity of recycled components decreases slightly from 0.72 to 0.70 in the proposed model compared to the model equipped with IoT. In contrast, the products transferred to disposal centers increase during these periods. In collection centers, disposal increases from 0.29 to 0.31. In remanufacturing centers, it increases from 0.19 to 0.21. In manufacturing centers, it increases from 0.19 to 0.22. As a result of the aforementioned changes, the inventory level of components in manufacturing centers decreases from 0.41 to 0.40. The inventory level of products in manufacturing centers decreases from 0.56 to 0.51. The inventory level of products in collection centers decreases from 0.38 to 0.35. In these periods, sales revenue improves by 51% with blockchain implementation, and total revenue from energy savings improves by 22%. Moreover, forward SC cost increases by 26%, and facility opening cost increases by 16%. Inventory holding cost increases by 39%, while the reverse logistics cost decreases by 55%. In contrast, in the proposed model, the fixed cost of implementing blockchain facilities accounts for 20% of the total cost. Under these conditions, the shortage cost decreases by 13.2%. Ultimately, the total SC profit increases by 60%, and the demand fulfillment rate increases by 1.7% compared to the model equipped with IoT.

Examining the trend of changes over 12 periods indicates that the significance of blockchain becomes prominent in periods with higher volumes of information interactions and return product flows. Specifically, the greatest impact of blockchain is observed in periods 6–9. During this interval, the proposed model reaches a saturation stage. The volume of production, return, and recycling activities is also at its highest level. As a result, reverse flows attain their maximum values. Under these conditions, transparent and reliable information recording becomes essential. In the proposed model, blockchain is assumed to provide transparent and reliable information records that support product traceability and information consistency across the network. These capabilities are intended to provide decision-makers with additional information for production, recycling, and distribution planning. Consistent with the computational results, the largest differences in profit and demand-fulfillment rate between the two models are observed during this period. In contrast, the least impact of blockchain is observed in periods 1–2 as well as 10–12; because the volume of network operations is lower and the flow of returned products is limited. During these periods, the differences between the proposed model and the IoT-only model become less pronounced because the volume of network operations and returned-product flows is lower. Consistent with the computational results, the difference between the two models in terms of cost and demand fulfillment decreases during these periods.

Discussion

To provide a structured interpretation of the results, this section is organized into four complementary perspectives. It begins with the theoretical contributions, highlighting how the findings extend existing knowledge on digitalization in CLSCs. It then discusses practical applications and managerial implications, translating the results into actionable insights for industry and decision-makers. Finally, the decision-support implications are presented, demonstrating how the proposed model can guide strategic and operational decisions under uncertainty.

Theoretical contributions

This study provides several non-trivial theoretical insights into the role of digital technologies in CLSCs. Contrary to the implicit assumption in much of the existing literature, digitalization dose not uniformly improve performance. The results reval that the benefits of IoT and blockchain are highly context-dependent. More pronounced differences between the compared scenarios emerge under conditions of high demand intensity and intensified return flows, where system complexity is greatest. This observation suggests that the value of digital technologies may depend on operational intensity and information uncertainty within the modeled environment. Furthermore, the information-visibility and traceability mechanisms incorporated in the proposed model are associated with distinct structural decisions, leading to more decentralized, dynamic, and responsive network configurations. These findings may challenge prevailing assumptions about static network design in closed-loop systems and provide a theoretical perspective on the potential role of information quality in structural adaptability.

Practical applications

The proposed framework has tangible applications for industries characterized by significant return flows and product recovery activities, such as electronics, automotive, and manufacturing systems with remanufacturing operations. The proposed model suggests that integrating real-time information from IoT and blockchain-enabled traceability may support better synchronization of forward and reverse flows, particularly in environments with volatile or peak demand. The computational results suggest that the proposed model may support improved coordination across production, distribution, collection, recycling, and remanufacturing processes. Within the modeled environment, this is associated with higher utilization of returned products, lower disposal quantities, and a more balanced allocation of operational resources.

Managerial implications

The results suggest that digital transformation may be viewed not as a uniform technological upgrade but as a strategic lever that modifies decision-making patterns across the SC. The increase in active facilities and expansion of both forward and reverse flows suggest that managers may consider more flexible network designs when operating under conditions similar to those represented in the model. Although the adoption of IoT and blockchain increases facility, inventory, and infrastructure costs, these investments are associated with lower shortage and reverse-logistics costs as well as higher revenues within the modeled environment. This highlights the importance of evaluating technology investments from a system-wide perspective rather than focusing exclusively on individual cost components. Moreover, the findings suggest that managers may benefit from selectively deploy digital technologies in periods or locations where demand intensity and return volumes are high, thereby maximizing the return on digital investment. Finally, improved information accuracy and traceability can facilitate recycling and remanufacturing activities, thereby supporting both environmental and economic sustainability objectives.

Decision-support implications

The proposed model can be used as a decision-support tool. It simultaneously evaluates multiple interdependent decisions under uncertainty. These decisions include facility activation, production and remanufacturing planning. They also include inventory management and technology investment. By capturing interactions between demand variability, return flows, and information quality, the framework can support managerial decision-making. It may help managers anticipate system behavior under uncertainty. The model also allows evaluation of alternative network configurations. This may support the design of more resilient and adaptive strategies. Therefore, the model may provide useful insights for real-world CLSCs characterized by uncertainty, complexity, and rapidly changing operating conditions.

Conclusion

In today’s competitive environment, mitigating potential threats and ensuring information security are recognized as essential requirements. On the one hand, utilizing IoT for real-time data collection and blockchain for transparent and immutable data recording has been recognized as a promising approach for supporting information security and operational visibility. Despite this, existing studies in the VCLSC literature have only considered the use of one of these technologies individually. Another issue that has received limited attention is demand uncertainty. To address these gaps, a multi-echelon, multi-period, and multi-product mathematical programming model was developed to maximize the profit of CLSC under fuzzy demand uncertainty.

Subsequently, in order to demonstrate the importance of utilizing blockchain and IoT technologies, two comparative studies were conducted. In the first study, the performance of the proposed model was compared with a similar model not equipped with IoT and blockchain technologies. The results of the comparison indicated two major changes in the values of decision variables: (1) an increase in the number of opened manufacturing, distribution, collection, remanufacturing, and disposal centers; (2) adjustments in operational flow decisions in accordance with demand variations across periods. Another key finding was that, within the modeled environment, the benefits associated with IoT and blockchain varied according to demand levels and operational intensity. The computational results indicate that the largest performance differences were observed during the middle periods. In these periods, demand and return volumes reached their highest levels. Under these conditions, the proposed model exhibited higher profit and demand-satisfaction rates, which were associated with improved traceability and information transparency. Furthermore, in the initial periods, the operational volume was limited. In the final periods due to the decline in demand, the impact of these technologies on profit and the percentage of demand satisfied was reduced. In the second study, the importance of using blockchain is examined; therefore, the developed model was compared with a similar model without blockchain technology. The comparison of results indicated that the trend of changes in the decision variable values was similar to that of the first comparative study, with the difference that the magnitude of differences in decision variable values between the two models was considerably greater in the first comparative study. Consequently, the largest differences between the proposed model and the IoT-only model were observed during the middle periods, while in initial and final periods, the additional benefits associated with blockchain were less evident.

Due to the NP-hard nature of the model, large-scale problems were solved using the GWO metaheuristic algorithm, with parameters tuned via the Taguchi method. To validate the developed GWO algorithm, its results were compared with those of the CPLEX solver in GAMS for small and medium-sized instances, showing high consistency. Moreover, GWO effectively solved large-scale problems within a reasonable time, whereas CPLEX faced time and memory limitations. These findings demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed approach for solving the large-scale problem instances considered in this study.

Considering the assumptions and scope of this study, future research can extend the model to a multi-objective framework incorporating economic, environmental, social, and service level considerations. Moreover, uncertainty conditions such as costs, product quality, lead time, and return rates can be incorporated. In this regard, the proposed model can be further developed toward stochastic programming or robust optimization frameworks to explicitly capture uncertainty and enhance real-world applicability. Additionally, emerging technologies such as artificial intelligence and machine learning can be integrated with IoT and blockchain to support intelligent demand forecasting, dynamic optimization, and the analysis of smart contracts in decentralized SCs. Furthermore, the model can be extended by considering dynamic decisions, as well as analyzing decentralized structures and competitive or cooperative interactions among facilities to provide deeper managerial insights into network coordination. Finally, future studies can analyze the long-term implications of these approaches for SC resilience and competitiveness, as well as examine cybersecurity and privacy issues.
